Massive gravity generalization of T ​ T ¯ T\overline{T} deformations
Evangelos Tsolakidis

Abstract
Motivated by the two-dimensional massive gravity description of T ​ T ¯ T\overline{T} deformations, we propose a direct generalization in d d dimensions. Our methodology indicates that all terms up to order d d are present in the deformation. In two dimensions, T ​ T ¯ T\overline{T} is enhanced by a linear and a constant term, and exhibits an interesting behaviour regarding the deformed spectrum and correlators. At certain limits, this deformation can reduce to T ​ T ¯ T\overline{T} or T ​ T ¯ + Λ 2 T\overline{T}+\Lambda_{2} consistently. Using the massive gravity method, we obtain the classically deformed action of a sigma model of bosons and fermions interacting with an arbitrary potential, extending previous results. As a consequence, a proposal regarding the deformation of higher-derivative theories is made. Moreover, a standard dimensional reduction procedure is presented, with the resulting operator matching the form of prior findings under certain assumptions. In d ≥ 2 d\geq 2 , we provide the exact structure of the quadratic terms in agreement with previous approaches, as well as the structure of the linear and constant terms. All higher order contributions are not easily evaluated, yet we derive the complete answer for all cases up to seven dimensions. Under certain conditions, these terms vanish, resulting in a quadratic operator. The trace-flow equation for this family of deformations is also derived. Finally, we investigate the class of root- T ​ T ¯ T\overline{T} operators in various dimensions within the scope of this formalism.

Contents

1  Introduction

The systematic study of irrelevant deformations in two dimensions was sparked by Zamolodchikov’s seminal discovery
[
1
]
, and was established as an independent field of research over a decade later due to crucial advancements
[
2
,
3
]
. One of the main goals of this field is to provide a better understanding of the space that quantum field theories live in. This is closely connected to the flow equation

\displaystyle\partialderivative{S}{\la}=\mathcal{O}\,,

(1.1)

where
S
represents the action,
\mathcal{O}
the deforming operator and
\lambda
the parameter of the deformation. Usually, the deforming operator is taken to be the determinant of the stress-tensor or
T\overline{T}
, due to its special quantum mechanical properties
[
1
]
.

Let us briefly motivate the equation above. The first thing we notice, is that (
1.1
) is equivalent to the standard way deformations are introduced under certain conditions. In more detail, if the deforming operator does not depend on
\lambda
it can be solved exactly yielding
S_{0}+\lambda\mathcal{O}
where
S_{0}
represents some seed quantum field theory. Clearly, when the deforming operator is a function of
\lambda
the solution is no longer trivial. The
T\overline{T}
operator falls into this category as it is constructed by the stress-tensor, which in turn is a function of the action
S
which implicitly depends on
\lambda
. Since this flow equation holds in both cases, we understand that it is more fundamental, in a sense that it is the governing equation for deformations. Considering all of the above, it is interesting to note that the set of non-trivial solutions to this equation is generated by operators that depend on the action in some way, with one possible choice being the corresponding conserved currents of
S
.

Since the discovery of the flow equation, various branches have sprouted in different research directions
[
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
]
. Notably,
T\overline{T}
can be understood as a random coordinate transformation
[
12
]
. Moreover, quite a few non-relativistic approaches exist
[
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
]
. Besides
T\overline{T}
, the deformations constructed by currents other than the stress-tensor have also been studied, with the most common one being a
U(1)
symmetry current
[
17
,
18
]
, as well as deformations involving square roots
[
19
,
20
,
21
,
22
]
. From a holographic point of view, a
T\overline{T}
-deformed conformal field theory on the boundary was found to be dual to
\text{AdS}_{3}
at finite radial cut-off
[
23
,
24
]
with various generalizations developed in
[
25
,
26
,
27
,
28
]
. Interestingly, the radial cut-off was found to be proportional to the inverse square of the deformation parameter. Another intriguing reformulation of
T\overline{T}
was discovered in
[
29
,
30
]
, where the deformed theory was found to be equivalent to flat space Jackiw-Teitelboim gravity coupled to the undeformed one. A curved background generalization exists
[
31
,
32
]
, where the undeformed action couples to massive gravity instead. Furthermore,
T\overline{T}
deformations of correlators have been investigated
[
27
,
33
,
34
,
35
,
36
]
. In higher dimensions, the structure of these stress-tensor deformations is an open problem, yet there are certain proposals of quadratic order
[
37
,
38
,
39
,
40
,
41
]
. Finally, a deformation in one dimension can be derived via dimensional reduction of
T\overline{T}

[
42
]
.

In this work, we will mostly focus on the massive gravity
1
1

1

For further details on massive gravity we refer the interested reader to
[
43
,
44
]
.
approach
[
31
,
32
]
, extending its formalism in
d
dimensions. We were motivated to adopt this method for the study of deformations, due to the fact that the seed theory does not need to be specified. We begin by developing the general methodology in
d
dimensions. By introducing a background and an auxiliary vielbein, we assume that the solution of (
1.1
) can be written as
S_{\text{G}}+S_{0}
, where
S_{\text{G}}
depends on both vielbeins and
\lambda
whereas
S_{0}
only on the auxiliary vielbein and other fields. Once a deformation (which is a function of the stress-tensor) is specified, the flow equation determines the exact expression for
S_{\text{G}}
. When
S_{\text{G}}+S_{0}
is evaluated on-shell for the auxiliary vielbein, the resulting expression is the deformed action. In
d=2
, we generalize the massive gravity method
[
31
,
32
]
by considering an operator quadratic in the stress-tensor, that reduces correctly to
T\overline{T}
as well as
T\overline{T}+\Lambda_{2}
in certain limits. The spectrum of this operator can be obtained, and an identification with a string theory spectrum is evident. Moreover, the structure of the linear term allows for the calculation of certain deformed correlators. A dimensional reduction of the deformation yields a one-dimensional operator, which agrees with previous proposals
[
42
]
under certain assumptions. As another application of this methodology, we provide an expression for the classically deformed sigma model of bosons and fermions interacting with an arbitrary potential, extending previous results. We find that the resulting expression allows for the deformation of certain higher-derivative theories. Finally, root-
T\overline{T}
is discussed from the massive gravity perspective. In a complete analogy to the two-dimensional case, we extend this approach in
d
dimensions. Specifying the deforming operator to be an order
d
polynomial in the stress-tensor, yields a quadratic structure that precisely matches previous treatments
[
37
,
38
]
. All higher order terms were too cumbersome to determine for general
d
, yet we do provide the complete expression of the deforming operator for all cases up to seven dimensions in appendix
E
. Lastly, we investigate the properties of root-
T\overline{T}
through the lens of massive gravity.

This paper is structured in the following way. In section
2
, we develop the massive gravity formalism in
d
dimensions. During section
3
, we apply our methodology in
d=2
recovering and partially extending many known results. The deforming operators in three, four and
d
dimensions are derived in section
4
, followed by a small discussion on root-
T\overline{T}
. Finally, in section
5
we provide a detailed summary of our work, along with some arguments on the potential constraints imposed on the stress-tensor, that result in an operator of quadratic order in
d
dimensions. We conclude with some discussions and a possible generalization of our method.

]
appeared on the arXiv, where partial overlap is noted with section
2
, as well as subsections
3.7
,
4.1
and
4.2
of our paper.

d
dimensions

C
for comparison reasons. Following the title of this section, we will temporarily work in
d
dimensions. As in
[
31
,
32
]
we will use two dimensionless vielbeins, the auxiliary

50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
. It will also be convenient to define the quantities
Y_{i}
and

(2.4)

which is the solution to the flow equation when evaluated on-shell for
\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
, at least classically. We will usually refer to the first term of the action above as the massive gravity action or
S_{\text{G}}
. The function
G
is any solution of the flow equation which takes the form of a first order non-linear
(d+1)
-dimensional partial differential equation and
S_{0}
is the seed action,
2
2

2

This action is arbitrary, it may contain curvature terms. The variable
\Phi
represents matter.
which is usually (but not always) the part that survives the
\lambda\rightarrow 0
limit. Also notice that
G
is restricted in the same way as
F
for the same reasons. Finally, it is evident that the nature of the seed action comes into play only when the equations of motion for the auxiliary vielbein are considered.

In order to better understand the origin of this solution consider a fixed
F
. Now, one can follow with the variation of (
2.4
) with respect to
\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
from which it is obvious that only the function
G
will survive. It is important to take these variations before evaluating everything on-shell for the auxiliary vielbein. A simple chain rule and we reach

\displaystyle W_{1}=G\mathds{1}_{d}-\sum_{j=1}^{d}j\partialderivative{G}{y_{j}}Y_{j}\,,

(2.5)

where
\mathds{1}_{d}
is the
d
-dimensional identity matrix, and

\displaystyle w_{i}=dG^{i}+\sum_{k=1}^{i}(-1)^{k}G^{i-k}\binom{i}{k}\sum_{j_{1},\dots,j_{k}=1}^{d}\quantity[\prod_{n=1}^{k}j_{n}\pdv{G}{y_{j_{n}}}]y_{j_{1}+\cdots+j_{k}}\,,

(2.6)

where
y_{j_{1}+\cdots+j_{k}}
can be written as a function of
(y_{1},\dots,y_{d})
, again from (
B.7
). Now assuming that the on-shell condition for the auxiliary vielbein holds, the flow equation (
1.1
) simply reads

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}G(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i},y_{1},\dots,y_{d})=F(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i},w_{1},\dots,w_{d})\,,

(2.7)

i.e. the first order non-linear
(d+1)
-dimensional partial differential equation for
G
. It is important to stress that a priori, one does not need to find the complete solution to (
2.7
), any function that satisfies it is a potential candidate. The major restriction to the set of possible solutions comes from the
\lambda\rightarrow 0
limit as well as dimensional analysis. The current understanding of the limit is simple, one recovers the seed action
S_{0}
on the
\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
background. In mathematical terms this translates to the following initial condition

G(0,\mathfrak{g}_{i},d,\dots,d)=0\,,

(2.8)

where the first equation should drop out naturally from the equations of motion for the auxiliary vielbein in that limit. During the next section we will see that one may relax the initial condition above in a specific way. Moreover, since the vielbeins are assumed to be dimensionless and
G
has mass units
M^{d}
, the explicit dependence on
\lambda
and
\mathfrak{g}_{i}
is fixed by dimensional analysis.

z_{i}\coloneqq\mathchoice{\quantity(Z_{i})^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{\quantity(Z_{i})^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{\quantity(Z_{i})^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{\quantity(Z_{i})^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}=\tr(Z_{i})\,,

(2.10)

and together with (
2.5
) we reach

\displaystyle f\bar{W}_{1}=f\bar{G}\mathds{1}_{d}+\bar{e}\bar{Z}_{1}\,.

(2.11)

The equation above essentially relates the deformed stress-tensor density with the undeformed one when the Einstein-Hilbert term is absent. As expected, when
\lambda\rightarrow 0
and (
2.8
) holds the stress-tensors match trivially.

To summarise, taking into consideration all of the above, we naturally reach the following, relatively simple algorithm: First, one begins by fixing
F
. Then the evaluation of
w_{i}
follows, using (
2.4
) and (
2.6
). This results in a first order non-linear
(d+1)
-dimensional partial differential equation for
G
i.e. (
2.7
), which in principle can be solved using e.g. the method of characteristics or an ansatz. In order to obtain the full solution, the initial condition (
2.8
) is used followed by the elimination of
\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
via (
2.9
). This step is classical, one may choose to perform the path integral over the auxiliary vielbein. Finally, one can apply this method iteratively. In more detail, for each iteration the background vielbein becomes the new auxiliary vielbein and the action becomes the new seed action. A more schematic explanation is presented in figure
1
bellow.

\overunderset{\mathcal{O}_{n,\lambda_{n}}}{\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}}{\xrightarrow{\hskip Figure 1
:
Iteration of
n
, not necessarily identical deformations using the massive gravity method. At each step one is free to integrate out all or part of the auxiliary vielbeins at will.

We will mostly work out the first four steps in various dimensions, leaving the rest for future endeavours.

3  Two dimensions

Shifting our attention to two dimensions, we will begin by deriving the deforming operator and check that at certain limits, one gets the correct massive gravity action for the well-studied
T\overline{T}
operator
[
31
,
32
]
, as well as the
T\overline{T}+\Lambda_{2}
operator
[
46
]
. Then, we will briefly study its spectrum and observe that it can be identified with a more general string theory spectrum. A small discussion about the deformed correlators of undeformed operators will follow, as well as a dimensional reduction to
d=1
. We will then derive the classically deformed theories for a sigma model of bosons and fermions interacting with an arbitrary potential in one and two dimensions and propose a way to extend the deformation to higher derivative theories. Finally, we will close this section with a study of root-
T\overline{T}

[
20
]
via the massive gravity method.

3.1
Quadratic deformations

We start from the generic solution (
2.4
), which now reads

\displaystyle S[\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}},\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}},\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i},\Phi]=\int\differential[2]{x}f\hskip 1.42271ptG(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i},y_{1},y_{2})+S_{0}[\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}},\mathfrak{g}_{i},\Phi]\,.

(3.1)

Following the first step of the algorithm, we specify the deformation (which is irrelevant in this case) to be of quadratic order i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}=\int\differential[2]{x}f\hskip 1.42271pt\quantity{b_{0}\tensor{T}{^\mu_\nu}\tensor{T}{^\nu_\mu}+b_{1}(\tensor{T}{^\mu_\mu})^{2}+\frac{b_{2}}{\la}\tensor{T}{^\mu_\mu}+\frac{b_{3}}{\la^{2}}}\,,

(3.2)

where the dependence
3
3

3

It is important to note that this choice is unique only when no other dimensionful couplings are present. It is understood that there do exist infinite combinations of more than one couplings that carry the correct units.
on
\lambda
is such that
b_{0},b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}
are dimensionless arbitrary constants. Then, (
2.7
) and (
3.2
) yield the corresponding flow equation, which we do not explicitly state since it is quite lengthy and does not contribute to our understanding. Nevertheless, we can still solve it through a quadratic ansatz for
G

\displaystyle G(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i},y_{1},y_{2})=c_{0}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})+c_{1}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{1}+c_{2}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{1}^{2}+c_{3}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{2}\,,

(3.3)

which yields the following system of equations

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}b_{0}(c_{3}+c_{2})=0\,,\quad\partial_{\lambda}c_{3}=b_{0}c_{1}^{2}\,,\quad\partial_{\lambda}c_{2}=b_{1}c_{1}^{2}\,,\quad\partial_{\lambda}c_{1}=\dfrac{c_{1}(2\lambda c_{0}(b_{0}+2b_{1})+b_{2})}{\lambda}\,,\\
\partial_{\lambda}c_{0}=\dfrac{2\lambda c_{0}(\lambda c_{0}(b_{0}+2b_{1})+b_{2})+b_{3}}{\lambda^{2}}\,,\end{gathered}

where the dependences have been dropped for notational economy. The system is overdetermined but the last four equations can be easily solved i.e.

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\begin{aligned} c_{0}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})&=\frac{b\quantity(\frac{2\mathfrak{c_{0}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})}{\la^{b}+\mathfrak{c_{0}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})}-1)-2b_{2}-1}{4\lambda\quantity(b_{0}+2b_{1})}\,,&c_{1}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})&=\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}^{b-1}\mathfrak{c_{1}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})}{\lambda^{b}+\mathfrak{c_{0}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})}\,,\\
c_{2}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})&=\mathfrak{c_{2}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})-\frac{b_{1}\mathfrak{c_{1}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})^{2}}{b\quantity(\la^{b}+\mathfrak{c_{0}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i}))}\,,&c_{3}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})&=\mathfrak{c_{3}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})-\frac{b_{0}\mathfrak{c_{1}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})^{2}}{b\quantity(\la^{b}+\mathfrak{c_{0}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i}))}\,,\end{aligned}\\[8.5359pt]
b\coloneqq\sqrt{(1+2b_{2})^{2}-8b_{3}(b_{0}+2b_{1})}\,,\end{gathered}

where
\mathfrak{c_{0}},\mathfrak{c_{1}},\mathfrak{c_{2}},\mathfrak{c_{3}}
are integration constants that depend exclusively on other couplings of the undeformed theory. Now, revisiting the first equation of (
3.1
) we distinguish two independent cases:
b_{0}=0
or
c_{3}=-c_{2}
. In the first case, the solution is given by (
3.1
) in that limit, but in the second, we get that

\displaystyle b_{1}=-b_{0}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{c_{3}}=-\mathfrak{c_{2}}\,.

(3.8)

With this choice and looking back to (
3.2
), we notice that the first two terms of the deformation are analogous to the
T\overline{T}
operator. During the next several subsections, we will focus exclusively on this case.

Let us now analyse the result above further. As previously mentioned in section
2
, the function
G
has mass units
M^{2}
in two dimensions, which in turn translates to the units of the coefficients
c_{i}
since the zweibeins are unitless. Furthermore for (
3.2
),
\lambda
has mass units
M^{-2}
therefore from (
3.1
) the units of
\mathfrak{c_{i}}
are uniquely determined or

\displaystyle\quantity[\mathfrak{c_{0}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})]=M^{-2b}\,,\quad\quantity[\mathfrak{c_{1}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})]=M^{1-b}\,,\quad\quantity[\mathfrak{c_{2}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})]=M^{2}\,,\quad\quantity[\mathfrak{c_{3}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})]=M^{2}\,,

(3.9)

which means that these constants are actually constrained functions of the other couplings of the seed theory.

3.1.1
Retrieving
T\overline{T}

Considering now (
3.8
), the standard massive gravity description of
T\overline{T}
naturally drops out when the coefficients
\mathfrak{c_{i}}
do not depend on other couplings i.e. they are dimensionless. The combination of this fact and (
3.9
) uniquely leads to
\mathfrak{c_{0}}=0=\mathfrak{c_{2}}
and
b=1
, which in turn translates to the following constraint

\displaystyle b_{3}=-\frac{b_{2}(1+b_{2})}{2b_{0}}\,.

(3.10)

The last thing that needs to be fixed is the constant
\mathfrak{c_{1}}
, which is determined simply via (
2.8
) in accordance with (
2.9
). We find that
b_{2}=0
and
\mathfrak{c_{1}}=-1/(2b_{0})
. In order to generalize this result let us relax (
2.8
) by allowing
b_{2}
to be arbitrary. Then, the massive gravity action reads

\displaystyle S_{\text{G}}=\int\differential[2]{x}\dfrac{f}{\lambda^{\prime}}\quantity{1+b_{2}-y_{1}+\det Y_{1}}=\int\differential[2]{x}\dfrac{f}{\lambda^{\prime}}\quantity{1+b_{2}-y_{1}}+\int\differential[2]{x}\dfrac{e}{\lambda^{\prime}}\,,

(3.11)

where
\lambda^{\prime}\coloneqq 2\lambda b_{0}
, and similarly (
3.2
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}=\int\differential[2]{x}f\hskip 1.42271pt2b_{0}\quantity{-\det\tensor{T}{^\mu_\nu}+\frac{b_{2}}{\la'}\tensor{T}{^\mu_\mu}-\frac{b_{2}(1+b_{2})}{\la'^{2}}}\,.

(3.12)

Now the
\lambda\rightarrow 0
limit is not well-defined, in fact it diverges. We will attempt to provide an explanation of this behaviour in the upcoming sections. Finally, turning off
b_{2}
and making use of (
A.2
) we retrieve

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{\text{G}}=\int\differential[2]{x}\dfrac{1}{2\lambda^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\epsilon^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\epsilon^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\epsilon^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\epsilon^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{\epsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}} (3.13)

in agreement with

3.1.2
Retrieving
T\overline{T}+\Lambda_{2}

Starting on the same footing as before, we pick the solution that obeys (
3.8
) but now
b_{2}=0
. Considering this, we will prove that at least one of the coefficients
\mathfrak{c_{i}}
carries units necessarily by contradiction. Let us assume that all
\mathfrak{c_{i}}
are unitless. Then, we once more have that
\mathfrak{c_{0}}=0=\mathfrak{c_{2}}
and
b=1
but now the condition for
b_{3}
is
b_{3}=-1/(8b_{0})
. For these choices there does not exist any non-zero value
4
4

4

If
\mathfrak{c_{1}}=0
, the deforming parameter completely decouples from the auxiliary zweibein, which is not compatible with our methodology.
of
\mathfrak{c_{1}}
such that
G
has the correct units.

From there, we understand that
G
will now be a function of other couplings as well, which may or may not be irrelevant
[
26
,
47
]
. In order for us to get
T\overline{T}+\Lambda_{2}
, we may now proceed with the following identification

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}b_{0}\longleftrightarrow\frac{1}{2\alpha}\,,\qquad b_{3}\longleftrightarrow\alpha\frac{\eta-1}{4}\,,\\
\mathfrak{c_{0}}(\lambda_{0})\longleftrightarrow-\lambda_{0}^{\sqrt{\eta}}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{c_{1}}(\lambda_{0})\longleftrightarrow-\alpha\sqrt{\eta}\sqrt{\lambda_{0}}^{\sqrt{\eta}-1}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{c_{2}}(\lambda_{0})\longleftrightarrow\alpha\frac{\sqrt{\eta}-1}{4\lambda_{0}}\,,\end{gathered}

where
\alpha
and
\eta
are arbitrary constants. This leads to the following massive gravity action

\displaystyle S_{\text{G}}=\int\differential[2]{x}\dfrac{\alpha}{2}\mathchoice{\epsilon^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\epsilon^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\epsilon^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\epsilon^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{\epsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}{b}}}}{\epsilon^{{\mathcho (3.16)

with

\displaystyle\beta_{\pm}(\lambda,\lambda_{0})\coloneqq 1\pm\sqrt{\eta}\coth(\frac{\sqrt{\eta}}{2}\log\frac{\lambda_{0}}{\lambda})\,,\quad\beta_{3}(\lambda,\lambda_{0})\coloneqq\sqrt{\eta}\csch(\frac{\sqrt{\eta}}{2}\log\frac{\la_{0}}{\la})\,,

(3.17)

in agreement with
[
46
]
. For (
3.2
), the term that comes with
b_{3}
in front is the one that is analogous to
\Lambda_{2}
. Setting
\eta=1
,
\alpha=1/(2b_{0})
and
\lambda_{0}=0
in that order, gives (
3.13
) as expected.

3.2
Spectrum analysis

It is rather interesting to explore the energy spectrum of the operator (
3.2
) when it is constrained only by (
3.8
) and placed on an Euclidean cylinder of circumference
R

[
1
]
. The details of this calculation are quite standard and are located in appendix
D
. In short, we find that the energies are given by

\displaystyle E^{\pm}_{n}(\lambda^{\prime},R)=-\dfrac{R\quantity(1+2b_{2})}{2\lambda^{\prime}}\pm\sqrt{\quantity(\dfrac{\mathcal{P}_{n}}{R})^{2}+\quantity(\dfrac{R\qty(1+2b_{2})}{2\la'})^{2}+\dfrac{2b_{0}\quantity(b_{3}R^{2}+\la'\mathcal{E}_{n})}{{\lambda^{\prime}}^{2}}}\,,

(3.18)

where
\mathcal{E}_{n}
are the undeformed energies that survive the
\lambda\rightarrow 0
limit and
\mathcal{P}_{n}=2\pi p_{n}
, with
p_{n}\in\mathds{Z}
. One notices that as in the standard
T\overline{T}
case, there are two branches for the energy and usually the divergent one gets discarded. We choose to keep both, as there are cases e.g.
[
48
,
49
,
50
]
where both branches are necessary. Taking now the
\lambda\rightarrow 0
limit, one retrieves a non-divergent value only for
b_{3}=0
and
b_{2}\neq-1/2
i.e.

\displaystyle E^{\pm}_{n}(R)=\frac{2b_{0}\mathcal{E}_{n}}{R(1+2b_{2})}\,,

(3.19)

where
b_{2}>-1/2
for the positive, and
b_{2}<-1/2
for the negative branch respectively. Keeping
b_{3}
at zero and defining
R^{\prime}\coloneqq R(1+2b_{2})
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{n}\coloneqq\mathcal{P}_{n}(1+2b_{2})
one gets back the standard
T\overline{T}
spectrum, but now the circumference of the cylinder and the momenta are scaled. Considering now the second constraint (
3.10
), we uniquely reach
b_{2}=0
–which is consistent with (
3.13
)– for the positive and
b_{2}=-1
for the negative branch.

The spectrum assumes a simpler form when we consider (
3.10
), with the terms related to the coefficient
b_{2}
cancelling exactly inside the square root, yielding

\displaystyle E^{\pm}_{n}(\lambda^{\prime},R)=-\dfrac{R\quantity(1+2b_{2})}{2\lambda^{\prime}}\pm\sqrt{\quantity(\dfrac{2\pi p_{n}}{R})^{2}+\quantity(\dfrac{R}{2\la'})^{2}+\dfrac{2b_{0}\mathcal{E}_{n}}{\lambda^{\prime}}}\,.

(3.20)

This equation is tantalisingly reminiscent of the string spectrum in
[
51
]
, which matches with our result up to the following non-unique identifications
5
5

5

For a different interpretation see
[
52
]
.

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}\leftrightarrow\dfrac{\alpha^{\prime}\pi}{w}\,,\quad b_{2}\leftrightarrow\dfrac{\mathchoice{B^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{0}{1}}}}{B^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{0}{1}}}}{B^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{0}{1}}}}{B^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{0}{1}}}}-1}{2}\,,\quad p_{n}\leftrightarrow\dfrac{N_{\text{L}}-N_{\text{R}}}{w}\,,\quad\mathcal{E}_{n}\leftrightarrow\dfrac{\pi\quantity(N_\text{L}+N_{\text{R}}+k_{\bot}^{2})}{wb_{0}}\,,

(3.21)

when
p_{\bot}^{2}=2k_{\bot}^{2}/\alpha^{\prime}
. Moreover, the
B
-field in similar fashion with
b_{2}
, cancels exactly inside the square root and this is consistent with the identification above. The units also seem to match, yet
b_{2}
is constant in contrast to
\mathchoice{B^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{0}{1}}}}{B^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{0}{1}}}}{B^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{0}{1}}}}{B^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{0}{1}}}}
, which is a function of the worldsheet coordinates, therefore this identification is limited to cases with a constant
B
-field. Finally, turning off the
B
-field, which corresponds to
b_{2}=-1/2
, we trivially retrieve a more familiar string theory spectrum
[
53
]
.

It is important to note that we retrieve known string theory spectra for certain choices of
b_{2}
that do not necessarily have a finite
\lambda\rightarrow 0
limit. This is due to the identification above, essentially
\lambda
plays the role of
\alpha^{\prime}
and consequently the string tension grows as the deformation parameter approaches zero. When we finally get there, the tension becomes infinite, followed by the energy and vice versa, in accordance with the derived spectrum. Regarding the analysis at the level of the action, this is a strong indicator towards the relaxation of the initial condition (
2.8
). Indeed, in subsection
3.5
we reach that conclusion.

Correlators of undeformed operators

Let us now shift our attention to correlators. For simplicity,

6

Technically, one should be able to begin this discussion from the general solution (
3.1
), potentially retrieving more properties regarding correlators. With our choice, we are essentially covering all deformations that depend explicitly only on
\lambda
.
consider the complete solution (
2.4
), the massive gravity action (
3.11
) and consequently the deformed partition function i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}=\int\mathcall{D}\Phi\hskip 0.56917pt\mathcall{D}e\hskip 2.84544pte^{iS_{\text{G}}[\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}},\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\tex }\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}},\mathfrak{g}_{i},\Phi]}=\int\mathcall{D}\Phi\hskip 2.84544pte^{iS[\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}},\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i},\Phi]}\,,

(3.22)

where the deforming operator is given by (
3.12
) and assuming that the path integral over the auxiliary zweibein can be performed. Staring at equation (
3.11
) for a bit leads to the following equality

\displaystyle\int\mathcall{D}\Phi\hskip 0.56917pt\mathcall{D}e\hskip 2.84544pt\exp(i\int\dd[2]{x}\dfrac{f}{\la'}\hspace{0.02cm}b_{2}+i\tilde{S}_{\text{G}}+iS_{0})=\int\mathcall{D}\Phi\hskip 2.84544pt\exp(i\int\dd[2]{x}\dfrac{f}{\la'}\hspace{0.02cm}b_{2}+iS_{T\overline{T}})\,,

(3.23)

where
\tilde{S}_{\text{G}}
is given by (
3.13
), i.e. the standard
T\overline{T}
massive gravity action and
S_{T\overline{T}}
is now the
T\overline{T}
deformed action. The role of
b_{2}
now becomes apparent, it behaves like a source term for the deformed theory. The only element missing is that
b_{2}
is constant and not an arbitrary function of spacetime. We can actually promote
b_{2}
to a function if we are careful in doing so. To make the argument clear, we briefly revisit equation (
3.3
) and (
3.1
), essentially the generic massive gravity solution to the operator (
3.2
). Now we boldly perform the following uplift

\displaystyle b_{i}\longrightarrow b_{i}(x)\,,\qquad i\in\{0,1,2,3\}\,,

(3.24)

and check if this affects any part of our algorithm. Retracing all the steps we took in the beginning of this section, we notice that this uplift is always valid unless any of the
b_{i}(x)
is a function of
\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
or/and
\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
or/and
\lambda
. That is because we essentially perform three types of variations throughout the process of finding the massive gravity solution: variations with respect to
\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
to calculate the deformed stress-tensor and consequently construct the deforming operator, variations with respect to
\lambda
for the flow equation and finally, variations with respect to
\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
in order to eliminate the auxiliary zweibein. As we mentioned in section
2
this last step is classical, but it also constraints the fully quantum mechanical approach in the same way. Following this, the source term can essentially be anything that does not couple to the background and does not depend on the deformation parameter. In other words, we are now able to calculate deformed correlators of undeformed operators.

Returning to (
3.22
), we now set
b_{2}(x)=J(x)\mathcal{O}(x)
(to be more general, we assume that
b_{0}(x)
is also a function) and define the
T\overline{T}
deformed correlators as follows

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\mathcal{O}(x_{1})\cdots\mathcal{O}(x_{n})}_{\lambda}\coloneqq\dfrac{\lambda^{\prime}(x_{1})\cdots\lambda^{\prime}(x_{n})}{i^{n}f(x_{1})\cdots f(x_{n})}\dfrac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}[J]}\dfrac{\delta\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}[J]}{\delta J(x_{1})\cdots\delta J(x_{n})}\Bigg|_{J=0}\,.

(3.25)

Furthermore, we can actually relate the deformed and undeformed correlators using (
3.23
), which takes the following neat form

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}[\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}]\expectationvalue{\mathcal{O}(x_{1})\cdots\mathcal{O}(x_{n})}_{\lambda}=\int\mathcall{D}e\hskip 2.84544pte^{i\tilde{S}_{\text{G}}[\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}},\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50636pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.24889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.24889pt][ (3.26)

where
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
is the undeformed partition function and the sources have been turned off. It is quite interesting to observe that the equation above looks like an integral transformation where the kernel is the exponential of
i\tilde{S}_{\text{G}}
. At
\lambda\rightarrow 0
the kernel essentially becomes a delta functional with support only at
\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248 and consequently the equality holds trivially as expected. Moreover, setting all operators to unity we recover the formula that relates the

3.4
Dimensional reduction, dilaton gravity theories and point particles

Another key aspect of
T\overline{T}
is the well-known trace-flow equation, that relates the trace of the deformed stress-tensor with its determinant classically. The combination of (
2.6
,
2.9
) and (
3.11
) leads to the following, more general, trace-flow equation

\displaystyle\dfrac{\bar{e}\tr\bar{\mathcall{S}}}{2}=\dfrac{f\tr\bar{T}}{2}-\lambda^{\prime}f\det\mathchoice{\bar{T}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{\bar{T}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{\bar{T}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{\bar{T}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}+fb_{2}\tr\bar{T}-\dfrac{fb_{2}(1+b_{2})}{\lambda^{\prime}}\,,

(3.27)

which will be the main actor of this subsection. We will always assume that we are on-shell for the auxiliary zweibein, thus we can safely drop the “bar” notation. In
[

]
, the authors begin from the standard
T\overline{T}
description and the corresponding flow equation for a seed conformal field theory, and dimensionally reduce via circular compactification of the spacial dimension with circumference set to one, followed by the trivial elimination of the anti-diagonal elements of the stress-tensor.

gives
