4  Higher dimensions

Motivated by the remarkable properties of the massive gravity method in two dimensions, we will now try to apply our methodology in higher dimensions. During this section, a lot of traces of powers of the stress-tensor will appear, hence we slightly switch our language adopting a matrix notation, leaving the indices behind. To avoid any misunderstanding, we will treat
\mathchoice{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}
as a matrix with the first index representing rows and the second columns. Furthermore, the coefficient
b_{0}
will appear extensively in our calculations, and at times, in the denominator of expressions, therefore we will always assume that it does not approach zero. This fact is verified a posteriori.

4.1
Deformations of order three in three dimensions

Looking back to (
3.2
) and (
3.3
), we found solutions for the most general quadratic operator using a quadratic ansatz for
G
. In a direct analogy, we now consider the most general cubic deformation i.e.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{O}=\int\differential[3]{x}f\hskip 1.42271pt\Bigg\{\lambda b_{0}\tr T^{3}+\lambda b_{1}\tr T^{2}\tr T+&\lambda b_{2}(\tr T)^{3}+\\
+b_{3}\tr T^{2}+&b_{4}(\tr T)^{2}+\dfrac{b_{5}}{\lambda}\tr T+\dfrac{b_{6}}{\lambda^{2}}\Bigg\}\,,\end{aligned}

(4.1)

where the dependence on
\lambda
is fixed such that all
b_{i}
are arbitrary and dimensionless. The mass units of
\lambda
are
M^{-3}
. The second ingredient is the ansatz for
G
, which we pick to be the most generic cubic polynomial in
(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})
or

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} G(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i},y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})=c_{0}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})+c_{1}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{1}+c_{2}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{1}^{2}+c_{3}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{2}+\\
+c_{4}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{1}^{3}+c_{5}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{1}y_{2}+c_{6}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{3}\,.\end{aligned}

(4.2)

From there, the massive gravity method yields the following solution

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\begin{aligned} c_{0}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})&=-\dfrac{4b_{3}}{3b_{0}\lambda}\,,\quad c_{1}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})=\mathfrak{c_{1}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})\lambda^{b}\,,\quad c_{2}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})=0=c_{3}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})\,,\quad\\
c_{4}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})&=\mathfrak{c_{4}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})-\dfrac{\mathfrak{c_{1}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})^{3}b_{0}}{4+6b}\lambda^{2+3b}\,,\quad c_{5}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})=-3c_{4}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})\,,\quad\\
c_{6}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})&=2c_{4}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})\,,\quad b\coloneqq 2b_{5}+\dfrac{4b_{3}^{2}}{3b_{0}}\,.\end{aligned}\end{gathered}

where
b_{1},b_{2},b_{4}
and
b_{6}
are now constrained,

\displaystyle b_{1}=-\dfrac{3b_{0}}{4}\,,\quad b_{2}=\dfrac{b_{0}}{8}\,,\quad b_{4}=-\dfrac{b_{3}}{2}\,,\quad b_{6}=4\dfrac{3b_{0}b_{3}+4b_{3}^{3}+9b_{0}b_{3}b_{5}}{9b_{0}^{2}}\,.

(4.4)

Surprisingly, the ratio
b_{4}/b_{3}
of the quadratic terms in the deformation, is equal to
-1/(3-1)
in agreement with
[
37
,
38
]
. We should also note that with this choice of
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
, the cubic order terms of the operator are not equal to the determinant of the stress-tensor. From a dimensional standpoint, since
G
has mass units
M^{3}
the dimensions of
\mathfrak{c_{1}}
and
\mathfrak{c_{4}}
are uniquely determined

\displaystyle\quantity[\mathfrak{c_{1}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})]=M^{3+3b}\,,\qquad\quantity[\mathfrak{c_{4}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})]=M^{3}\,.

(4.5)

In order to simplify things, let us consider the case where no other couplings are present, which translates to the condition
b=-1
and
\mathfrak{c_{4}}=0
. This gives yet another constraint

\displaystyle b_{5}=-\dfrac{1}{2}-\dfrac{2b_{3}^{2}}{3b_{0}}\,,

(4.6)

thus the massive gravity action assumes a familiar form (
3.11
)

\displaystyle S_{\text{G}}=\int\differential[3]{x}\dfrac{f}{\lambda^{\prime}}\quantity{-4b_{3}+3b_{0}\mathfrak{c_{1}}y_{1}+(3b_{0})^{2}\mathfrak{c_{1}}^{\!\!\!3}\det Y_{1}}\,,

(4.7)

where
\lambda^{\prime}\coloneqq 3\lambda b_{0}
. Once again, this theory can be fully determined by applying the initial condition (
2.8
), which fixes
\mathfrak{c_{1}}
exactly. Moreover, we observe that the last term in the equation above, completely decouples from the background as in the two-dimensional case. The full solution now reads

\displaystyle S[\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}},\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}},\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i},\Phi]=\int\differential[3]{x}\dfrac{f}{\lambda^{\prime}}\quantity{3b_{0}\mathfrak{c_{1}}y_{1}-4b_{3}}+(3b_{0})^{2}\mathfrak{c_{1}}^{\!\!\!3}\int\differential[3]{x}\dfrac{e}{\lambda^{\prime}}+S_{0}[\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}},\mathfrak{g}_{i},\Phi]\,,

(4.8)

where one notices that, essentially, all the dynamics of the gravity action reduce to just
y_{1}
, whereas the seed theory absorbs a constant term proportional to the inverse of
\lambda^{\prime}
. We must note that this is a simple rewriting of the terms and it does not change the flow equation or the deforming operator. A first quick calculation regarding the operator (
4.1
) is to find its trace-flow equation. For the massive gravity solution (
4.7
) and using (
2.6
,
2.9
) we retrieve

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\bar{e}\tr\bar{\mathcall{S}}}{3}=\dfrac{f\tr\bar{T}}{3}+f\lambda\Bigg\{\lambda b_{0}\tr\bar{T}^{3}+\lambda b_{1}\tr\bar{T}^{2}\tr\bar{T}+&\lambda b_{2}(\tr\bar{T})^{3}+\\
+b_{3}\tr\bar{T}^{2}+&b_{4}(\tr\bar{T})^{2}+\dfrac{b_{5}}{\lambda}\tr\bar{T}+\dfrac{b_{6}}{\lambda^{2}}\Bigg\}\,,\end{aligned}

(4.9)

therefore if the seed theory does not contain any dimensionful couplings, the combination of (
4.1
) and (
4.9
) leads to the following flow equation

\displaystyle\derivative{S}{\la}=-\dfrac{1}{3\lambda}\int\differential[3]{x}f\hskip 1.42271pt\tr\bar{T}=\dfrac{1}{\Delta_{\lambda}\lambda}\int\differential[3]{x}f\hskip 1.42271pt\tr\bar{T}\,,

(4.10)

when we are on-shell for the auxiliary dreibein, where
\Delta_{\lambda}
is the mass dimension of
\lambda
. This is exactly what one would expect for a theory with only one dimensionful coupling.

Following this analysis, we would like to explore if more choices exist for the
b_{i}
which are not predicted by the massive gravity method. In order to achieve this, we will consider a simple Nambu-Goto boson in
d
-dimensions described by the following action

\displaystyle S=\int\differential[d]{x}\dfrac{f}{\xi\lambda}\quantity{\rho-\sqrt{1+\xi\la\partial^{\mu}\phi\partial_{\mu}\phi}}\,,

(4.11)

where
\xi
and
\rho
are dimensionless constants, and check if it is a solution of the deforming operator (
4.1
) for
d=3
. Interestingly, we find that this model is indeed flowing with respect to this deformation for the following choices of
b_{i}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} b_{0}&=\dfrac{\xi^{2}}{6}\,,\qquad b_{1}=-\dfrac{\xi^{2}}{4}\,,\qquad b_{2}=\dfrac{\xi^{2}}{12}\,,\qquad b_{3}=\dfrac{\rho\xi}{4}\,,\qquad\\
b_{4}&=-\dfrac{\rho\xi}{4}\,,\qquad b_{5}=\dfrac{1}{2}(\rho^{2}-1)\,,\qquad b_{6}=\dfrac{\rho}{2\xi}(1-\rho^{2})\,.\qquad\end{aligned}

(4.12)

This solution is not consistent with the constraints (
4.4
) and (
4.6
) therefore we conclude that more deformations of cubic order with the same structure as in (
4.1
) exist.

4.2
Deformations of order four in four dimensions

Let us now repeat the same process in four dimensions, with the hope of extracting the general behaviour of the deformation in arbitrary dimensions. We begin from an ansatz of order four for the deformation

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{O}=\int\differential[4]{x}f\hskip 1.42271pt\Bigg\{\lambda^{2}b_{0}\tr T^{4}+\lambda^{2}b_{1}\tr T^{3}\tr T+\lambda^{2}b_{2}(\tr T^{2})^{2}+\lambda^{2}b_{3}\tr T^{2}(\tr T)^{2}+&\\
+\lambda^{2}b_{4}(\tr T)^{4}+\lambda b_{5}\tr T^{3}+\lambda b_{6}\tr T^{2}\tr T+\lambda b_{7}(\tr T)^{3}+&\\
+b_{8}\tr T^{2}+b_{9}(\tr T)^{2}+\dfrac{b_{10}}{\lambda}\tr T+\dfrac{b_{11}}{\lambda^{2}}&\Bigg\}\,,\end{aligned}

(4.13)

where all
b_{i}
are unitless. The mass units of
\lambda
in this case are
M^{-4}
and
M^{4}
for
G
. Looking for solutions of quartic form

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} G(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i},y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},y_{4})=c_{0}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})+c_{1}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{1}+c_{2}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{1}^{2}+c_{3}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{2}+&\\
+c_{4}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{1}^{3}+c_{5}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{1}y_{2}+c_{6}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{3}+c_{7}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{1}^{4}+&\\
+c_{8}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{1}^{2}y_{2}+c_{9}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{1}y_{3}+c_{10}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{2}^{2}+c_{11}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})y_{4}\,,\end{aligned}

(4.14)

we find

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\begin{aligned} c_{0}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})&=-\dfrac{9b_{5}}{4\lambda b_{0}}\,,\quad c_{1}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})=\mathfrak{c_{1}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})\lambda^{b}\,,\quad c_{j}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})=0\,,\quad j=\{2,3,4,5,6\}\,,\\
c_{7}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})&=\mathfrak{c_{7}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})-\dfrac{\mathfrak{c_{1}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})^{4}b_{0}}{6(3+4b)}\lambda^{3+4b}\,,\quad c_{8}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})=-6c_{7}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})=c_{11}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})\,,\quad\\
\end{aligned}\\
c_{9}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})=8c_{7}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})\,,\quad c_{10}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})=3c_{7}(\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i})\,,\quad b\coloneqq 3b_{10}+\dfrac{27b_{5}^{3}}{16b_{0}^{2}}\,,\end{gathered}

where many of the
b_{i}
are constrained in the following way

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} b_{1}=-\dfrac{8b_{0}}{9}\,,\quad b_{2}=-\dfrac{b_{0}}{2}\,,\quad b_{3}=\dfrac{5b_{0}}{9}\,,\quad b_{4}=-\dfrac{11b_{0}}{162}\,,\quad b_{6}=-\dfrac{b_{5}}{2}\,,\quad\\
b_{7}=\dfrac{b_{5}}{18}\,,\quad b_{8}=\dfrac{9b_{5}^{2}}{8b_{0}}\,,\quad b_{9}=-\dfrac{3b_{5}^{2}}{8b_{0}}\,,\quad b_{11}=9\dfrac{27b_{5}^{4}+16b_{0}^{2}b_{5}(1+4b_{10})}{64b_{0}^{3}}.\end{aligned}

(4.17)

From the constraints above, we notice that the ratio
b_{9}/b_{8}
is equal to
-1/(4-1)
confirming once more
[
37
,
38
]
. Moreover, the choice above does not imply that the quartic order terms are proportional to the determinant. The units of
\mathfrak{c_{1}}
and
\mathfrak{c_{7}}
are given by

\displaystyle\quantity[\mathfrak{c_{1}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})]=M^{4+4b}\,,\qquad\quantity[\mathfrak{c_{7}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})]=M^{4}\,.\quad

(4.18)

Furthermore, assuming that
\lambda
is the only dimensionful coupling,
b=-1
and
\mathfrak{c_{7}}=0
just like the three-dimensional case. The extra constraint now reads

\displaystyle b_{10}=-\dfrac{1}{3}-\dfrac{9b_{5}^{3}}{16b_{0}^{2}}\,,

(4.19)

and the massive gravity action follows

\displaystyle S_{\text{G}}=\int\differential[4]{x}\dfrac{f}{\lambda^{\prime}}\quantity{-9b_{5}+4b_{0}\mathfrak{c_{1}}y_{1}+(4b_{0})^{2}\mathfrak{c_{1}}^{\!\!\!4}\det Y_{1}}\,,

(4.20)

where
\lambda^{\prime}\coloneqq 4\lambda b_{0}
and
\mathfrak{c_{1}}
is fixed by (
2.8
).
The full solution becomes

\displaystyle S[\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}},\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}},\lambda,\mathfrak{g}_{i},\Phi]=\int\differential[4]{x}\dfrac{f}{\lambda^{\prime}}\quantity{4b_{0}\mathfrak{c_{1}}y_{1}-9b_{5}}+(4b_{0})^{2}\mathfrak{c_{1}}^{\!\!\!4}\int\differential[4]{x}\dfrac{e}{\lambda^{\prime}}+S_{0}[\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}},\mathfrak{g}_{i},\Phi]\,,

(4.21)

where the same behaviour as in three dimensions is noted. We proceed with the trace-flow equation, which assumes the following familiar form

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \dfrac{\bar{e}\tr\bar{\mathcall{S}}}{4}=\dfrac{f\tr\bar{T}}{4}+f\lambda\Bigg\{\lambda^{2}b_{0}\tr\bar{T}^{4}+\lambda^{2}b_{1}\tr\bar{T}^{3}\tr\bar{T}+\lambda^{2}b_{2}(\tr\bar{T}^{2})^{2}+\lambda^{2}b_{3}\tr\bar{T}^{2}(\tr\bar{T})^{2}+&\\
+\lambda^{2}b_{4}(\tr\bar{T})^{4}+\lambda b_{5}\tr\bar{T}^{3}+\lambda b_{6}\tr\bar{T}^{2}\tr\bar{T}+\lambda b_{7}(\tr\bar{T})^{3}+&\\
+b_{8}\tr\bar{T}^{2}+b_{9}(\tr\bar{T})^{2}+\dfrac{b_{10}}{\lambda}\tr\bar{T}+\dfrac{b_{11}}{\lambda^{2}}\Bigg\}\,,&\end{aligned}

(4.22)

therefore when the auxiliary vierbein is eliminated and
\lambda
is the only dimensionful coupling, the four-dimensional flow equation is

\displaystyle\derivative{S}{\la}=-\dfrac{1}{4\lambda}\int\differential[4]{x}f\hskip 1.42271pt\tr\bar{T}=\dfrac{1}{\Delta_{\lambda}\lambda}\int\differential[4]{x}f\hskip 1.42271pt\tr\bar{T}\,,

(4.23)

where yet again,
\Delta_{\lambda}
is the mass dimension of
\lambda
as expected.

Lastly, let us check which are the solutions that (
4.11
) predicts at
d=4
given the operator (
4.13
). We find that
b_{0},b_{1}
and
b_{2}
are unconstrained, therefore we can freely set them to zero. This choice conveniently leads to
b_{3}=0=b_{4}
, which removes the quartic order terms entirely from the operator. The surviving coefficients read

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} b_{5}&=\dfrac{\xi^{2}}{8}\,,\qquad b_{6}=-\dfrac{\xi^{2}}{6}\,,\qquad b_{7}=\dfrac{\xi^{2}}{24}\,,\qquad b_{8}=\dfrac{7\rho\xi}{24}\,,\qquad\\
b_{9}&=-\dfrac{\rho\xi}{6}\,,\qquad b_{10}=\dfrac{3}{8}(\rho^{2}-1)\,,\qquad b_{11}=\dfrac{\rho}{2\xi}(1-\rho^{2})\,.\qquad\end{aligned}

(4.24)

Once again, the coefficients above do not match with (
4.17
) and (
4.19
), which suggests the existence of other operators with the same form besides (
4.13
).

The analysis of these two sections is quite tedious, but provides a crucial insight to the structure of these deformations which is necessary for the uplift in
d
dimensions.

4.3
Five, six, seven and
d
dimensions

Contrary to the title of this section, we will begin the discussion directly in
d
dimensions, and then confirm everything in dimension five, six and seven. Let us first fix the mass units of
\lambda
to be
M^{-d}
in accordance with every section that precedes this one and revisit equations (
4.5
) and (
4.18
). We assume that in
d
dimensions there does exist a similar set of two
\mathfrak{c_{i}}
which we will refer to as
\mathfrak{c_{1}}
and
\mathfrak{c_{\star}}
with dimensions

\displaystyle\quantity[\mathfrak{c_{1}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})]=M^{d+db}\,,\qquad\quantity[\mathfrak{c_{\star}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})]=M^{d}\,.

(4.25)

where
b
is a complicated function of
b_{i}
and
d
. Looking at the dimensionality of
\mathfrak{c_{\star}}
, we understand that there are only four ways it can be part of the massive gravity action. The first possibility is the simplest one, it just enters only by coupling to
y_{i}
. The other options are: an appropriate function of
(\lambda,\mathfrak{c_{\star}})
or
(\mathfrak{c_{1}},\mathfrak{c_{\star}})
or
(\lambda,\mathfrak{c_{1}},\mathfrak{c_{\star}})
, all coupled to
y_{i}
. A natural assumption is that the deformation should be the same for any seed theory carrying the couplings
\mathfrak{g}_{i}
including theories where
\mathfrak{g}_{i}\rightarrow 0
. The only choice that is consistent with that limit is the first one, and it also constrains the way it couples to
y_{i}
. We deduce that
\mathfrak{c_{\star}}
enters the
d
-dimensional massive gravity solution in the following way

\displaystyle\int\differential[d]{x}f\hskip 1.42271ptd!\mathfrak{c_{\star}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})\det Y_{1}=\int\differential[d]{x}e\hskip 1.42271ptd!\mathfrak{c_{\star}}(\mathfrak{g}_{i})\,,

(4.26)

which agrees with our previous treatments in two, three and four dimensions. From there it follows that
\mathfrak{c_{\star}}
essentially represents the seed theory
S_{0}
. To make this argument more transparent, recall that all
\mathfrak{c}_{i}
were introduced as integration constants with respect to
\lambda
which means that they can, in principle, depend on the local coordinates. We now understand better the form of the generic solution (
2.4
), which is consistent with the argument above. Since we are interested only in the massive gravity part of the solution we can safely turn off
\mathfrak{c_{\star}}
for the rest of this section.

The situation is not that simple for
\mathfrak{c_{1}}
. Using the arguments above,
\mathfrak{c_{1}}
cannot appear on its own, we expect some coupling to
\lambda
through a unit-appropriate function. Nevertheless, we can however say more when the initial condition (
2.8
) is imposed. In that case
\mathfrak{c_{1}}
is a dimensionless constant and
b=-1
. Revisiting (
4.7
) and (
4.20
), the last thing that we need to fix is the appearance of the constant term. We find that in the
d
-dimensional case, if one extends the deformation structure in precisely the same way, this constant is proportional to
b_{p(d)}
where
p(d)
is equal to the integer partitions of
d
, and is controlling the term
\tr T^{d-1}
. Combining all of the above, we propose the following solution in
d
-dimensions

\displaystyle S_{\text{G}}=\int\differential[d]{x}\dfrac{f}{\lambda^{\prime}}\quantity{-(d-1)^{2}b_{p(d)}+db_{0}\mathfrak{c_{1}}y_{1}+(db_{0})^{2}\mathfrak{c_{1}}^{\!\!\!d}\det Y_{1}}\,,\qquad d\geq 3\,,

(4.27)

where
\lambda^{\prime}\coloneqq d\lambda b_{0}
. Using this solution, the ratio
r
of the quadratic coefficients of the deformation is fixed, as well as the linear and constant coefficients. In more detail, let
\beta_{l}
be the coefficient of
\tr T
and
\beta_{c}
of the constant term of the deformation. Then, crucially,
r=-1/(d-1)
in agreement with
[
37
,
38
]
, and

\displaystyle\beta_{l}=\dfrac{1}{1-d}\quantity(1+\dfrac{[(d-1)b_{p(d)}]^{d-1}}{[db_{0}]^{d-2}})\,,\qquad\beta_{c}=\dfrac{(1-d)^{2}b_{p(d)}}{db_{0}}\beta_{l}\,,\qquad d\geq 3\,.

(4.28)

The higher order coefficients of the deformation are unique functions of
b_{0}
and
b_{p(d)}
, and can be evaluated by specifying the dimension, but a closed form expression is eluding us. It is rather odd that the massive gravity solution can be derived easier than the deformation itself, which is what we are interested in at the end of the day. Let us stop for a moment to comment on the last two equations. The main observation is the condition
d\geq 3
which is not obvious, since we are free to set
d=2
. This choice provides only a very specific solution in two dimensions and this is why we exclude it. In more detail setting
d=2
we observe that interestingly, (
4.28
) yields (
3.10
) with
\beta_{c}\leftrightarrow b_{3}
as expected, but it constraints
b_{2}
specifically at the value
b_{2}=-1/2
, which is the limit that removes the overall constant from the spectrum (
3.18
). Looking only at (
4.27
) for
\mathfrak{c_{1}}=-1/(2b_{0})
, and comparing with (
3.11
) we get the same constraint. Considering these facts, we conclude that (
4.27
) does not cover the entire span of solutions in two dimensions, hence the condition
d\geq 3
. There is however another interpretation in which the massive gravity solution (
4.27
) is more fundamental, meaning that in two dimensions,
b_{2}
must be equal to
-1/2
. In that case, the solution is valid for
d\geq 2
.

In order to better understand the structure of the coefficients of the higher order terms in the deformation, we leave
d=2
behind us and assume that
d\geq 3
. Moreover, in order to highlight the importance of the quadratic terms, and to avoid using the awkward coefficient
b_{p(d)}
, we define
\beta
as

\displaystyle b_{p(d)}\eqqcolon\dfrac{(2\beta)^{\frac{1}{d-2}}(db_{0})^{\frac{d-3}{d-2}}}{d-1}\,,

(4.29)

with (
4.27
) assuming the following form

\displaystyle S_{\text{G}}=\int\differential[d]{x}\dfrac{f}{\lambda^{\prime}}\quantity{-(2\beta)^{\frac{1}{d-2}}(d-1)(d b_{0})^{\frac{d-3}{d-2}}+d b_{0}\mathfrak{c_{1}}y_{1}+(d b_{0})^{2}\mathfrak{c_{1}}^{\!\!\!d}\det Y_{1}}\,,

(4.30)

and the coefficients
\beta_{l},\beta_{c}
given by (
4.28
), become

\displaystyle\beta_{l}=\dfrac{1+(2\beta)^{\frac{1}{d-2}}(db_{0})^{\frac{d-3}{d-2}}}{1-d}\,,\qquad\beta_{c}=-(d-1)\beta_{l}\dfrac{1+(d-1)\beta_{l}}{2\beta}\,.

(4.31)

The first thing that we notice is that
\beta_{c}
has a similar structure as in (
3.10
) with the only difference being that
\beta_{l}
is multiplied by
(d-1)
. Furthermore, the choice (
4.29
) brings the deforming operator in the following form

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}=\int\differential[d]{x}f\hskip 1.42271pt\Bigg\{\text{higher order terms}+\beta\quantity(\tr T^{2}-\dfrac{1}{d-1}(\tr T)^{2})+\dfrac{\beta_{l}}{\lambda}\tr T+\dfrac{\beta_{c}}{\lambda^{2}}\Bigg\}\,,

(4.32)

where the quadratic terms are in agreement with
[
37
,
38
]
. As we mentioned earlier, the higher order terms are uniquely specified by inserting the solution and the operator above in the flow equation. For all practical purposes, and in order to verify that the massive gravity action (
4.30
) is indeed valid, the deforming operators in five, six and seven dimensions as well as their higher order coefficients are given in appendix
E
. Another property of the operator above emerges if one compares (
3.27
,
4.9
) and (
4.22
). It should not come as a surprise that the trace-flow equation now reads

\displaystyle\dfrac{\bar{e}\tr\bar{\mathcall{S}}}{d}=\dfrac{f\tr\bar{T}}{d}+f\lambda\Bigg\{\text{$\overline{\hbox{higher order terms}}$}+\beta\quantity(\tr\bar{T}^{2}-\dfrac{1}{d-1}(\tr\bar{T})^{2})+\dfrac{\beta_{l}}{\lambda}\tr\bar{T}+\dfrac{\beta_{c}}{\lambda^{2}}\Bigg\}\,,

(4.33)

where the auxiliary vielbein is on-shell. A quick check in higher dimensions verifies that this equation holds. Then, turning off all couplings except
\lambda
returns the following flow equation

\displaystyle\derivative{S}{\la}=-\dfrac{1}{d\lambda}\int\differential[d]{x}f\hskip 1.42271pt\tr\bar{T}=\dfrac{1}{\Delta_{\lambda}\lambda}\int\differential[d]{x}f\hskip 1.42271pt\tr\bar{T}\,,

(4.34)

with
\Delta_{\lambda}
being the mass dimension of
\lambda
.

Let us attempt to investigate which are the potential necessary conditions for the stress-tensor, such that all higher order terms vanish. In
[
37
,
38
]
, the constraint
8

G
is taken to be the massive gravity density.

is extensively used therefore, this would be our starting point. This implies that

(4.38)

This is precisely the behaviour we observe. Assuming now that (
4.36
) holds for every
d\geq 2
, all higher order terms vanish identically, leaving us only with the quadratic deformation. We should also note that in the last iteration of this process we recover the two-dimensional solution (
3.12
), with
b_{2}=-1-4b_{0}/3
. Moreover, the recursive relation (
4.37
) for the stress-tensor can be solved yielding

3.54399pt{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-17.96622pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.23616pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.23616pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.2611pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.47223pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.54399pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(2)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.23616pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.23616pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.2611pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.47223pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}T^{{\kern-7.11476pt{(2)}\kern 2.53142pt{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-16.95364pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.23616pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.23616pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.2611pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.47223pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.53142pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}\,,

(4.39)

where the initial condition is located at
d=2
as we discussed. This equation suggests that a hidden two-dimensional stress tensor is the source of the deformation. A more heuristic argument for these choices will be presented during the discussion in section
5
.

4.4
Root deformations

For this final subsection, we will consider the following root-
T\overline{T}
operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\int\differential[d]{x}f\hskip 1.42271ptb_{\text{r}}\sqrt{\mathchoice{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}\mathchoice{T^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86 (4.40)

in direct analogy with (
3.58
), and use the massive gravity method in order to understand its behaviour. Our guiding principle is the ModMax theory in four dimensions
[
61
,
62
]
, which is part of the flow of the operator above at
d=4

[
19
,
63
,
21
]
. The Lagrangian density for this model is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{ModMax}}=\mathcal{L}_{\text{Max}}\cosh(b_{\text{r}}\m)+\sqrt{\mathcal{L}_{\text{Max}}^{2}+\mathcal{L}_{\text{p}}^{2}}\sinh(b_{\text{r}}\m)\,,

(4.41)

where
\mathcal{L}_{\text{Max}}\coloneqq-\tr F^{2}/4
and
\mathcal{L}_{p}\coloneqq-\tr(F\star F)/4
, with
\mathchoice{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}
being the usual electromagnetic tensor and
\mathchoice{(\star F)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{(\star F)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{(\star F)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{(\star F)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}
its Hodge-dual. We observe that only the two aforementioned variables are present, which indicates that out of the set of variables
y_{i}
(
2.1
), with
1\leq i\leq d
, for our massive gravity formalism, there must be choice such that only two survive. Taking into account that
\mathchoice{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}
is antisymmetric, we are naturally lead to an antisymmetric choice for
Y_{1}
which in turn restricts the massive gravity variables to
y_{2i}
with
1\leq i\leq\lfloor d/2\rfloor
. The two-variable choice and the floor function, restrict the number of dimensions to
d=4
or
d=5
. In both cases, the surviving variables are
y_{2}
and
y_{4}
, yet their flow equations for (
4.40
) are drastically different. Specifically at
d=5
, we were not able to find any solutions. Settling for four dimensions, the flow equation assumes the following familiar form

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}G=b_{\text{r}}\sqrt{4y_{4}-y_{2}^{2}}(\partial_{y_{2}}G+y_{2}\partial_{y_{4}}G)\,,

(4.42)

where
b_{\text{r}}
has absorbed a
\pm
factor just like in the two-dimensional case. The equation above can be solved exactly

\displaystyle G(\mu,\mathfrak{g}_{i},y_{2},y_{4})=\mathcall{G}\quantity[\mathfrak{g}_{i},y_{2}^{2}-2y_{4},\mu b_{\text{r}}+\arctanh\qty(y_{2}^{-1}\sqrt{4y_{4}-y_{2}^{2}})]\,,

(4.43)

for any function
\mathcall{G}
, which is strikingly similar to (
3.60
). We again observe that the deformation parameter decouples from the massive gravity variables, therefore the same arguments as in subsection
3.7
apply. This should also indicate the presence of “seed” massive gravity, which in turn means that the uplift
y_{2}\rightarrow y_{2,\mu}
and
y_{4}\rightarrow y_{4,\mu}
should exist. Remarkably, all these statements are true since the solution can be brought in the following form

\displaystyle G(\mu,\mathfrak{g}_{i},y_{2},y_{4})=\mathcall{G}\quantity(\mathfrak{g}_{i},y_{2,\m},y_{4,\m})\,,

(4.44)

where

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &y_{2,\mu}=y_{2}\cosh(b_{\text{r}}\m)+\sqrt{4y_{4}-y_{2}^{2}}\sinh(b_{\text{r}}\m)\,,\\
&y_{4,\mu}=y_{4}\cosh(2b_{\text{r}}\m)+\dfrac{y_{2}}{2}\sqrt{4y_{4}-y_{2}^{2}}\sinh(2b_{\text{r}}\m)\,.\end{aligned}

(4.45)

Moreover, the second argument in (
4.43
) indicates that
2y_{4,\mu}-y_{2,\mu}^{2}=2y_{4}-y_{2}^{2}
, which is verified easily using the equations above. The similarities do not stop there, we notice that (
4.45
) remains invariant under
y_{2}\leftrightarrow y_{2,\mu}
and
y_{4}\leftrightarrow y_{4,\mu}
, therefore by defining the vierbein
\mathchoice{\varepsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\varepsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\varepsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\varepsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
such that

\displaystyle y_{2}^{\star}\coloneqq\mathchoice{f^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{f^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{f^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{f^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}\mathchoice{\varepsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}_{{{\lambda}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\varepsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.743 t][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}}}{f^{{{\lambda}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}}}\mathchoice{\varepsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\varepsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\varepsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\varepsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyl style$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{h}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=y_{4,\mu}\,,

(4.46)

one should be able to derive expressions that are equivalent to (
3.66
) and (
3.67
) in four dimensions. We cannot help but notice that there does exist a set of equations given by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\chi_{n,\mu}=\chi_{n}\cosh(b_{\text{r}}\m)+\sqrt{2n\chi_{m}-\chi_{n}^{2}}\sinh(b_{\text{r}}\m)\,,\\
&\chi_{m,\mu}=\chi_{m}\cosh(2b_{\text{r}}\m)+\dfrac{\chi_{n}}{n}\sqrt{2n\chi_{m}-\chi_{n}^{2}}\sinh(2b_{\text{r}}\m)\,,\end{aligned}

(4.47)

such that
n\chi_{m,\mu}-\chi_{n,\mu}^{2}=n\chi_{m}-\chi_{n}^{2}
and remains invariant under the change
\chi_{n}\leftrightarrow\chi_{n,\mu}
and
\chi_{m}\leftrightarrow\chi_{m,\mu}
. It would be interesting to explore if this generalization can be useful.

Let us now close this section by making the connection to the matter seed theory in four dimensions. If this type of deformation is universal, one would expect that upon correct identification of the generic scalars
x_{2}
and
x_{4}
in the seed theory (
C.3
), the deformation should be given by the set (
4.45
). Surprisingly, identifying
x_{2}\leftrightarrow-\tr F^{2}/4
and
x_{4}\leftrightarrow\tr F^{4}/16
returns (
4.41
). It is then clear that the antisymmetry of
\mathchoice{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}
implies the antisymmetry of
Y_{1}
and vice versa.

5  Summary and discussion

In this work, we extended the massive gravity method in arbitrary dimensions. We began with a detailed explanation of the methodology for general
d
. Briefly, we introduced the background and auxiliary vielbeins and a simple yet powerful reformulation of the deformed action in a massive gravity part constructed by the two kinds of vielbeins and a seed theory part, which depends exclusively on matter and the auxiliary vielbein. Once the deformation and the massive gravity action are specified, the auxiliary vielbein is integrated out, either classically or quantum mechanically. This approach comes in direct analogy to the well-studied two-dimensional case
[
31
,
32
]
.

In terms of direct applications, setting our starting point in two dimensions, we derived the most general quadratic massive gravity solution using the most general ansatz of the same order as the deforming operator. We observed that the nature of the solution constrained the coefficients of the quadratic terms of the deforming operator to be exactly equal to
T\overline{T}
, with the coefficient in front of the linear term being unaffected by that choice, something which was also true for the constant term. Then, we showed that
T\overline{T}
and
T\overline{T}+\Lambda_{2}
naturally drop out at certain limits of this deformation, indicating its generality. The next stop of this journey was the spectrum analysis of this generalized operator when no other dimensionful couplings were present. In this limit the operator is given by (
3.12
). The spectrum (
3.18
) was found to be compatible with a string theory spectrum enriched by a
B
-field contribution under certain identifications. From there, the source of this extra term was found to essentially be the linear and constant terms that are present in our operator and can be turned off for the choice
b_{2}=-1/2
. Notably, for this choice the limit
\lambda\rightarrow 0
is no longer well-defined.

Another interesting property of (
3.12
) was unveiled in subsection
3.3
. In short, we found that the coefficient in front of the linear term, can be uplifted to a source term for the deformed theory, allowing one to evaluate
T\overline{T}
-deformed correlators of undeformed operators either directly from the deformed partition function (
3.25
) or via the undeformed one (
3.26
). It would be instructive to compare our results with other approaches
[
27
,
33
,
34
,
35
,
36
]
. Motivated by the uplift of the linear coefficient, we dimensionally reduced the operator using the generalized trace-flow equation (
3.27
) and matched it with the operator obtained by the dimensional reduction of a dilaton gravity action with an arbitrary potential
[
42
]
under certain non-trivial assumptions. The precise matching conditions are given by (
3.33
). Following this, the one dimensional massive gravity action was evaluated using the aforementioned methodology and a system of bosons and fermions was deformed yielding results consistent with the literature
[
42
]
. Returning to two dimensions, a similar classical system was deformed
[
3
,
27
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
8
]
yielding the more transparent expression (
3.50
). The first point we would like to stress further is that, in the process of deforming this type of seed actions we noticed that the intrinsic properties of matter do not enter the analysis, therefore one should only consider the specific way fields couple to the background. Furthermore, we showed that the deformation of the aforementioned model essentially allows for the deformation of higher-derivative theories. Finally, we studied the class of root-
T\overline{T}
deformations, finding that its structure does not admit a specific massive gravity solution, but enhances the solutions of other deformations (
3.64
). After some manipulations, one can “push” this deformation on to the seed theory (
3.68
), verifying that a massive gravity description is not necessarily needed.

In higher dimensions, we began by deriving the operator in three and four dimensions, and used the solutions as our guiding principle. Then, an exact massive gravity prescription was found in
d
dimensions which reads

\displaystyle S_{\text{G}}=\int\differential[d]{x}\dfrac{f}{\lambda^{\prime}}\quantity{-(2\beta)^{\frac{1}{d-2}}(d-1)(d b_{0})^{\frac{d-3}{d-2}}+d b_{0}\mathfrak{c_{1}}y_{1}+(d b_{0})^{2}\mathfrak{c_{1}}^{\!\!\!d}\det Y_{1}}\,,

(5.1)

where
Y_{1}
is given by (
2.1
), followed by the corresponding operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}=\int\differential[d]{x}f\hskip 1.42271pt\Bigg\{\text{higher order terms}+\beta\quantity(\tr T^{2}-\dfrac{1}{d-1}(\tr T)^{2})+\dfrac{\beta_{l}}{\lambda}\tr T+\dfrac{\beta_{c}}{\lambda^{2}}\Bigg\}\,.

(5.2)

The higher order terms proved to be too cumbersome to determine for general
d
, yet its quadratic structure precisely agrees with previous results
[
37
,
38
]
. Moreover, for
d=2
, we recover the operator (
3.12
) with
b_{2}=-1/2
, which is a choice that we found to be interesting. As a check, we derived the full solution up to seven dimensions. Following this, the generalized trace-flow equation (
4.33
) is given, and a simple calculation (
4.34
) verifies its validity. In an effort to reduce the order of the deformation, we found that the choice

) suggests that this is true for any

(d-1)

(d-1)

d

normal directions in the way that we described. The last direction of research in this paper was the study of root-

T\overline{T}
in two dimensions is located at

in agreement with previous approaches

]
.

Let us briefly stress once more the importance of equations (
4.25
). During the early stages of this work, we assumed that the solution of the flow equation (
1.1
) assumes the form
S_{\text{G}}+S_{0}
, where the
\lambda\rightarrow 0
limit returns only
S_{0}
evaluated on the background
\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
. Notice that these are two conditions, essentially given by (
2.8
), one of which exclusively for
S_{\text{G}}
. Since the massive gravity density satisfies the
(d+1)
-dimensional partial differential equation (
2.7
) and there is only one initial condition, one is restricted to the set of solutions that are fully determined by it. This is precisely the content of (
4.25
), with the constant
\mathfrak{c_{1}}
representing the initial condition for
S_{\text{G}}
and
\mathfrak{c_{\star}}
for
S_{0}
.

Finally, we would like to make some concluding remarks about a potential generalization of our method. For the entirety of this work, we worked with operators that depended exclusively on conserved currents of translational symmetry i.e. the stress-tensor. This choice indicates that the background and auxiliary vielbeins must be used in the formulation of
S_{\text{G}}
, since the stress tensor is generated by the variation of the action with respect to them. If some other conserved current, say
J
, is used as part of the deformation, the auxiliary gauge field that generates
J
should be introduced in
S_{\text{G}}
, where G now stands for gauge field. In two dimensions this is already true
[
31
]
, even in the non-relativistic limit
[
16
]
, and there is no obvious reason why this approach would not work in higher dimensions.

Appendix A
Conventions

Most of our notation agrees with
[
64
]
. Everything in this section lives in
d
dimensions and we will always use the
(-,+,\dots,+)
signature for the metric. Let indices
M_{i}
be either curved
(M_{i}\rightarrow\mu_{i})
or flat
(M_{i}\rightarrow m_{i})
. The (anti)symmetrization of a tensor

carrying
n

. We also make use of the following identity

(A.2)

Please note that the symbol with the indices up is independent from the one with the indices down. In order to relate the two one needs to define the Levi-Civita tensor as follows

(A.3)

The Clifford algebra is given by
\{\mathchoice{\gamma^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\gamma^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\gamma^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\gamma^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}},\mathchoice{\gamma^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\gamma^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\gamma^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\gamma^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\}=2\mathchoice{\eta^{{{a}{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\eta^{{{a}{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\eta^{{{a}{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\eta^{{{a}{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathds{1}_{d}
, where
\mathds{1}_{d}
is the

-dimensional identity matrix. The

and

is the spin connection. Finally,
A\vecev{\nabla}_{\mu}B\coloneqq A\vec{\nabla}_{\mu}B-A\cev{\nabla}_{\mu}B
.

Appendix B
Characteristic and elementary symmetric polynomial(s)

The characteristic polynomial for any
d\times d
matrix
A
is given by

\displaystyle\det(\rho\mathds{1}_{d}-A)=\sum_{n=0}^{d}c_{n}\rho^{d-n}\,,\qquad c_{n}=(-1)^{n}e_{n}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\dots,\rho_{d})\,,

(B.1)

where
\mathds{1}_{d}
is the
d
-dimensional identity matrix,
\rho_{k}
is the
k^{\text{th}}
eigenvalue of
A
and
e_{n}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\dots,\rho_{d})
is the
n^{\text{th}}
elementary symmetric polynomial which is defined as

\displaystyle e_{n}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\dots,\rho_{d})\coloneqq\sum_{1\leqslant j_{1}<j_{2}<\cdots<j_{n}\leqslant d}\rho_{j_{1}}\rho_{j_{2}}\cdots\rho_{j_{n}}\,,\qquad n\leq d\,,

(B.2)

with
e_{0}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\dots,\rho_{d})=1
and
e_{n>d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\dots,\rho_{d})=0
. While the expressions above provide a way to calculate a determinant of a
d\times d
matrix as a finite sum containing
d
terms, it is still a highly unpractical way to do so. In order to make life simpler consider the rank of the matrix
A
and the subsequent rank-nullity theorem, which in our case simply states that
\rank(A-\rho\mathds{1}_{d})+\gamma_{A}(\rho)=d
, where
\gamma_{A}(\rho)
is the geometric multiplicity associated with the eigenvalue
\rho
. Another useful fact is that
\gamma_{A}(\rho)\leq\mu_{A}(\rho)\leq d
where
\mu_{A}(\rho)
is the algebraic multiplicity of
\rho
. Combining these two, we get that for
\rho=0
the following is true

\displaystyle d-\rank A\leq\mu_{A}(0)\,,

(B.3)

which in simple words translates to: the matrix
A
has at least
d-\rank A
zero eigenvalues. Considering that

\displaystyle d=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mu_{A}(\rho_{i})=\mu_{A}(0)+\mu_{A}(\rho\neq 0)\,,\qquad\mu_{A}(\rho\neq 0)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mu_{A}(\rho_{i})(1-\delta_{\rho_{i},0})\,,

(B.4)

where
k\leq d
is counting the distinct eigenvalues of
A
, (
B.3
) becomes

\displaystyle\mu_{A}(\rho\neq 0)\leq\rank A\,,

(B.5)

which means that, generally speaking, the maximum number of non-zero eigenvalues of the matrix
A
is equal to
\rank A
. Now, we revisit (
B.2
) and we notice that for any
n
such that
\rank A<n
, we obtain
e_{n}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\dots,\rho_{d})=0
. This is because we would have a product of
n
eigenvalues out of which only
\rank A
are non-zero in the best case for each term of the
n
sums over the eigenvalues. This allows us to rewrite (
B.1
) in a much more “convenient” form

\displaystyle\det(\rho\mathds{1}_{d}-A)=\sum_{n=0}^{\rank A}c_{n}\rho^{d-n}\,,\qquad c_{n}=(-1)^{n}e_{n}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\dots,\rho_{d})\,,

(B.6)

where the series truncates significantly for small
\rank A
. Finally, the Cayley-Hamilton theorem reads

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\rank A}c_{n}A^{d-n}=0\,,

(B.7)

which will be used extensively in this work.

Appendix C
The ansatz method

In this appendix, we will briefly review and marginally extend the ansatz method in
d
dimensions for comparison reasons. The standard flow equation at the action level reads

\displaystyle\partialderivative{S}{\la}=\mathcal{O}\,,

(C.1)

where
\lambda
is the deformation parameter and
\mathcal{O}
represents any deformation. A common starting ground includes Poincaré invariance, as well as minimally coupled to a non-singular background
\mathchoice{g^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{g^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{g^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{g^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}
theories. This leads to the well-known definition for the stress-tensor

\displaystyle\mathchoice{T^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{T^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{T^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{T^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}\coloneqq\frac{-2}{\sqrt{-g}}\frac{\delta (C.2)

A natural assumption regarding the deformed theory is that for
\lambda\rightarrow 0
one should retrieve some known seed Poincaré invariant density
\mathcal{L}_{0}
. A suitable general ansatz for the density
\mathcal{L}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{i},\dots,x_{D})\,,

(C.3)

where
D\geqslant d
is a positive integer. The index
i
that is running from
0
to
D
is a non-negative integer and is counting the number of inverse metrics that the corresponding (Lorentz scalar) element
x_{i}
contains. In other words

\displaystyle x_{i}\coloneqq\text{all possible contractions of}\phantom{a}\mathchoice{X^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.81232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.81232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.20248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.35892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95927pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95927pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.67052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.97893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.67499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.05043pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.05043pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.65828pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81471pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.19739pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.19739pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.12631pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.43472pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu_{1}}{\nu_{1}}{\cdots}{\mu_{i}}{\nu_{i}}}}}{X^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.81232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.81232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.20248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.35892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95927pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95927pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.67052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.97893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.67499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.05043pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.05043pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.65828pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81471pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.19739pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.19739pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.12631pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.43472pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu_{1}}{\nu_{1}}{\cdots}{\mu_{i}}{\nu_{i}}}}}{X^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.81232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.81232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.20248pt][c
